afternoons at the museum or lose myself in photography and cooking, but lately I look at my camera or the bridge table and feel absolutely nothing except the effort it would take to pretend.\n- key: S028\n  name: Psychomotor_Changes\n  description: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.\n  manifestation: My wife says I either pace through the house shuffling papers like I am late for a board meeting, or I sit so still at the kitchen table that it takes me a long time to answer a simple question.\n- key: S030\n  name: Worthlessness_Guilt\n  description: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).\n  manifestation: I keep replaying the investment decision and telling myself I have ruined my wife’s security and made a fool of a lifetime of being the responsible one.\n- key: S032\n  name: Death_Suicide\n  description: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.\n  manifestation: There are mornings when I think it would be a relief if I simply did not wake up, and I frightened myself enough to tell my wife I should not be left brilliantly optimistic, as if every obstacle was trivial, but if anyone questioned me I snapped at them like they were trying to sabotage the only sensible man in the room.\n- key: S034\n  name: Increased_Activity\n  description: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.\n  manifestation: I had this engine running inside me that made sitting quietly feel impossible, so I was up before sunrise calling people, rewriting proposals, and moving from one task to another without stopping.\n- key: S035\n  name: Inflated_Self_Esteem\n  description: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).\n  manifestation: I truly believed I could repair my retirement accounts, advise three start-ups, and turn the arts council into a regional powerhouse, as though my judgment was suddenly superior to everyone else’s.\n- key: S037\n  name: Pressured_Speech\n  description: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.\n  manifestation: At meetings I would hear myself talking over people and continuing long after their eyes glazed over, but silence felt intolerable and I could not seem to hand the conversation back.\n- key: S038\n  name: Racing_Thoughts\n  description: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.\n  manifestation: My mind would jump from portfolio strategy to donors to museum exhibits to some new business idea so fast that I could barely finish one sentence before the next one took over.\n- key: S040\n  name: Increased_Goal_Activity\n For a few weeks I felt almost… unusually optimistic, like everything was going to work out and any problem was easy to get past. But I was also quick to get angry if someone questioned me. I’d snap at them, like they were the ones being unreasonable.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes. I used to spend whole afternoons at the museum, or get absorbed in photography or cooking. Lately I’ll look at my camera, or even think about sitting down to play bridge, and I just feel… nothing. Mostly it just feels like it would take effort to pretend I’m enjoying it.\n\nDoctor: How long did this extremely optimistic feeling last?\n\nPatient: It lasted for weeks. I don’t remember the exact number, but it wasn’t just a day or two — I felt almost brilliantly optimistic for quite a while, like every problem was small. And during that same stretch, if anyone questioned me, I’d snap at them.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever feel so good or hyper that you got into trouble for being too outgoing or reckless?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose so. I became much more… active than usual. I started mentoring several companies at once, writing up fundraising ideas, setting up lunches with half the town. In retirement, that’s way more than I’d normally take on.\n\nDoctor: Did you notice your extremely optimistic feelings switching back and forth with periods where you felt really down?\n\nPatient: Yes, it did feel like it went back and forth. For weeks I’d feel almost brilliantly optimistic, like every obstacle was nothing, and then I’d hit stretches where I felt really down. During that high period, if anyone questioned me, I’d snap at them, like they were getting in the way or trying to undermine me.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period of at least two weeks where you felt sad or empty most of the day?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s been much longer than two weeks. Since those plans fell apart, I’ve been feeling low and guilty and mostly withdrawn… about nine months now.\n\nDoctor: Did your periods of feeling extremely optimistic and your periods of feeling really down tend to happen one after the other?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I can tell they were different periods, but I don’t know if they always came right one after the other in a clear pattern. It’s a bit hard for me to line it up exactly.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I felt like I had an engine running inside me. Sitting still was almost impossible, so I’d be up before sunrise calling people, rewriting proposals, jumping from one thing to the next without really stopping.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I’d call it sad. I haven’t noticed feeling especially down, no. It’s more that I feel worn out and unsettled.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I’ve felt keyed up and unsettled, but I’m not sure I’d call it anxiety exactly.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling hopeless about your future?\n\nPatient: Yes… I guess I have. I keep going over that investment decision in my head, telling myself I’ve ruined my wife’s security. Like I made a fool of myself after a whole lifetime of trying to be the responsible one.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt extremely high or euphoric?\n\nPatient: Yes. For a few weeks I felt almost… unusually optimistic, like everything was going to work out and any problem was easy to get past. But I was also quick to get angry if someone questioned me. I’d snap at them, like they were the ones being unreasonable.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes. I used to spend whole afternoons at the museum, or get absorbed in photography or cooking. Lately I’ll look at my camera, or even think about sitting down to play bridge, and I just feel… nothing. Mostly it just feels like it would take effort to pretend I’m enjoying it.\n\nDoctor: How long did this extremely optimistic feeling last?\n\nPatient: It lasted for weeks. I don’t remember the exact number, but it wasn’t just a day or two — I felt almost brilliantly optimistic for quite a while, like every problem was small. And during that same stretch, if anyone questioned me, I’d snap at them.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever feel so good or hyper that you got into trouble for being too outgoing or reckless?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose so. I became much more… active than usual. I started mentoring several companies at once, writing up fundraising ideas, setting up lunches with half the town. In retirement, that’s way more than I’d normally take on.\n\nDoctor: Did you notice your extremely optimistic feelings switching back and forth with periods where you felt really down?\n\nPatient: Yes, it did feel like it went back and forth. For weeks I’d feel almost brilliantly optimistic, like every obstacle was nothing, and then I’d hit stretches where I felt really down. During that high period, if anyone questioned me, I’d snap at them, like they were getting in the way or trying to undermine me.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period of at least two weeks where you felt sad or empty most of the day?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s been much longer than two weeks. Since those plans fell apart, I’ve been feeling low and guilty and mostly withdrawn… about nine months now.\n\nDoctor: Did your periods of feeling extremely optimistic and your periods of feeling really down tend to happen one after the other?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I can tell they were different periods, but I don’t know if they always came right one after the other in a clear pattern. It’s a bit hard for me to line it up exactly.\n\nDoctor: Did you notice that after feeling really down, you would then feel extremely optimistic again, in a kind of cycle?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think that did happen. After being really down, there were times when I’d swing back into feeling almost brilliantly optimistic for weeks, like nothing could really stop me. But I’d also get very irritable if anyone questioned me, like they were trying to get in my way.\n\nDoctor: Did your periods of feeling extremely optimistic and your periods of feeling really down happen in a regular pattern?\n\nPatient: I can’t say it was regular, no. I noticed the ups and downs, but I don’t think they followed a clear schedule or pattern that I could predict.\n\nDoctor: Did your periods of feeling extremely optimistic and your periods of feeling really down both occur within the past year?\n\nPatient: Yes, within the past year. After the investment loss, for about five months I really wasn’t living like my normal retired self. Everything was kind of centered on trying to undo what happened.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Like I made a fool of myself after a whole lifetime of trying to be the responsible one.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt extremely high or euphoric?\n\nPatient: Yes. For a few weeks I felt almost… unusually optimistic, like everything was going to work out and any problem was easy to get past. But I was also quick to get angry if someone questioned me. I’d snap at them, like they were the ones being unreasonable.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes. I used to spend whole afternoons at the museum, or get absorbed in photography or cooking. Lately I’ll look at my camera, or even think about sitting down to play bridge, and I just feel… nothing. Mostly it just feels like it would take effort to pretend I’m enjoying it.\n\nDoctor: How long did this extremely optimistic feeling last?\n\nPatient: It lasted for weeks. I don’t remember the exact number, but it wasn’t just a day or two — I felt almost brilliantly optimistic for quite a while, like every problem was small. And during that same stretch, if anyone questioned me, I’d snap at them.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever feel so good or hyper that you got into trouble for being too outgoing or reckless?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose so. I became much more… active than usual. I started mentoring several companies at once, writing up fundraising ideas, setting up lunches with half the town. In retirement, that’s way more than I’d normally take on.\n\nDoctor: Did you notice your extremely optimistic feelings switching back and forth with periods where you felt really down?\n\nPatient: Yes, it did feel like it went back and forth. For weeks I’d feel almost brilliantly optimistic, like every obstacle was nothing, and then I’d hit stretches where I felt really down. During that high period, if anyone questioned me, I’d snap at them, like they were getting in the way or trying to undermine me.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period of at least two weeks where you felt sad or empty most of the day?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s been much longer than two weeks. Since those plans fell apart, I’ve been feeling low and guilty and mostly withdrawn… about nine months now.\n\nDoctor: Did your periods of feeling extremely optimistic and your periods of feeling really down tend to happen one after the other?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I can tell they were different periods, but I don’t know if they always came right one after the other in a clear pattern. It’s a bit hard for me to line it up exactly.\n\nDoctor: Did you notice that after feeling really down, you would then feel extremely optimistic again, in a kind of cycle?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think that did happen. After being really down, there were times when I’d swing back into feeling almost brilliantly optimistic for weeks, like nothing could really stop me. But I’d also get very irritable if anyone questioned me, like they were trying to get in my way.\n\nDoctor: Did your periods of feeling extremely optimistic and your periods of feeling really down happen in a regular pattern?\n\nPatient: I can’t say it was regular, no. I noticed the ups and downs, but I don’t think they followed a clear schedule or pattern that I could predict.\n\nDoctor: Did your periods of feeling extremely optimistic and your periods of feeling really down both occur within the past year?\n\nPatient: Yes, within the past year. After the investment loss, for about five months I really wasn’t living like my normal retired self. Everything was kind of centered on trying to undo what happened.\n\nDoctor: Did your periods of feeling extremely optimistic usually last about the same amount of time?\n\nPatient: Not really about the same each time, no. The main stretch I remember clearly was after the investment loss — for about five months I wasn’t living like my usual retired self, and everything was focused on trying to undo the damage.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
